(110)

The accelerated expansion corresponds to negative values of
q
,
q<0
. Hence the condition for the acceleration expansion in Herglotz-type
f(R,T)
gravity reads

\mathchoice{\rho^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.13435pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.81026pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{eff}}}}{\rho^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.13435pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.81026pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{eff}}}}{\rho^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.13435pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.81026pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{eff}}}}{\rho^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.13435pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.81026pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{eff}}}}+3\mathchoice{p^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.13435pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.81026pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{eff}}}}{p^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.13435pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.81026pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{eff}}}}{p^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.13435pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.81026pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{eff}}}}{p^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.13435pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.81026pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{eff}}}}+\rho+3p<0.

(111)

In the particular case of a pressureless fluid (dust) we have
p=0
, and the deceleration parameter becomes

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}\frac{\left(8\pi+\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.79965pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.79965pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.54399pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53142pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{T}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.79965pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.79965pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.54399pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53142pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{T}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.79965pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.79965pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.54399pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53142pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{T}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.79965pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.79965pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.54399pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53142pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{T}}}}\right)\rho+\mathchoice{\ddot{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{\ddot{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{\ddot{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{\ddot{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}-(2\phi+H)\mathchoice{\dot{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{\dot{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{\dot{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{\dot{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}-(\dot{\phi}+\phi^{2}-\phi H)\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}}{\left(8\pi+\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.79965pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.79965pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.54399pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53142pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{T}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.79965pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.79965pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.54399pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53142pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{T}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.79965pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.79965pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.54399pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53142pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{T}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.79965pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.79965pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.54399pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53142pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{T}}}}\right)\rho+3\phi H\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}-3H\mathchoice{\dot{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{\dot{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{\dot{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{\dot{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}+(\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}R-f)/2}

(112)

\displaystyle-1.

The acceleration condition is then given as

\displaystyle\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}R-f-\left(8\pi+\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.79965pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.79965pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.54399pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53142pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{T}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.79965pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.79965pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.54399pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53142pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{T}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.79965pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.79965pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.54399pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53142pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{T}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.79965pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.79965pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.54399pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53142pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{T}}}}\right)\rho>

\displaystyle 3\left(\mathchoice{\ddot{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{\ddot{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{\ddot{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{\ddot{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}-2\phi\mathchoice{\dot{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{\dot{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{\dot{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{\dot{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}+H\mathchoice{\dot{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{\dot{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{\dot{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{\dot{f}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}-\dot{\phi}\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}+\phi^{2}\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}-\phi H\mathchoice{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}{f^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.06009pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75835pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.68454pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{R}}}}\right).

(113)

IV.2
Specific cosmological models

In the models considered below, we assume the Universe is filled with a pressureless fluid (dust), setting
p=0
. Under this assumption, the trace of the energy-momentum tensor reduces to
T=-\rho
.

IV.2.1
Model I:
f(R,T)=R+\alpha T

We begin by analyzing one of the most simple extensions to general relativity, choosing
f(R,T)=R+\alpha T
where
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. In this case the FLRW equations read

\displaystyle 3H^{2}

\displaystyle=3\phi H+\frac{1}{2}(16\pi+3\alpha)\rho,

(114)

\displaystyle 2\dot{H}+3H^{2}

\displaystyle=\dot{\phi}+2\phi H-\phi^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{2}\rho.

(115)

Eq. (
114
) is an algebraic constraint, which can be used to isolate the density function. Replacing this in the dynamical equation gives

2\dot{H}-\dot{\phi}=2\phi H-3H^{2}-\phi^{2}+\frac{3\alpha}{16\pi+3\alpha}(H^{2}-\phi H),

(116)

or, equivalently,

\dot{H}=\frac{\dot{\phi}}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\phi H-\frac{\phi^{2}}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha+8\pi\right)\rho.

(117)

The deceleration parameter is obtained as

q=-\frac{3}{2}\left[\frac{\dot{\phi}-\phi H-\phi^{2}-(\alpha+8\pi)\rho}{3\phi H+\left(16\pi+3\alpha\right)\rho/2}\right]-1.

(118)

The condition for accelerated expansion gives for the matter density the constraint

\frac{8\pi\rho}{3}<\dot{\phi}-\phi^{2}+\phi H.

(119)

The dynamical equations involve two unknown functions, so an additional constraint is required to close the system. It is also convenient to introduce a set of dimensionless variables,

\tau=H_{0}t,~H=H_{0}h,~\rho=\frac{3H_{0}^{2}}{8\pi}r,~\phi=H_{0}\Phi,~\alpha=\frac{16\pi}{3}A,

(120)

where
H_{0}
represents the present-day value of the Hubble constant. Thus, we have the dynamical equation

\displaystyle 2\frac{dh(\tau)}{d\tau}-\frac{d\Phi(\tau)}{d\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2+A}{1+A}\Phi(\tau)h(\tau)-\Phi^{2}(\tau)

(121)

\displaystyle-\frac{3+2A}{1+A}h^{2}(\tau),

supplemented by the algebraic constraint

h^{2}(t)=\Phi(\tau)h(\tau)+(1+A)r(\tau).

(122)

Analytical solutions.

When considering a simple linear model, there are two ways to obtain analytical solutions. These occur when either the Hubble or the Herglotz functions are a constant, that is
h(\tau)=h_{0}
or
\Phi(\tau)=\Phi_{0}
with
h_{0},\Phi_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. The former case corresponds to a de Sitter type solution, whereas the latter case can be interpreted as a situation in which a constant is a source of the non-conservation of energy and momentum.

Aiming to obtain a de Sitter solution, Eq. (
121
) reads

\frac{d\Phi(\tau)}{d\tau}=\Phi^{2}(\tau)-\frac{(2+A)h_{0}}{1+A}\Phi(\tau)+\frac{(3+2A)h_{0}^{2}}{1+A}.

(123)

This is an integrable differential equation, and it is a particular form of the Riccati equation. In order to simplify the equation we introduce the notations
\sigma=(2+A)h_{0}/(2+2A)
, and
\psi=(3+2A)h_{0}^{2}/(1+A)
, respectively, which allow us to write it as

\frac{d\Phi(\tau)}{d\tau}=\Phi^{2}(\tau)-2\sigma\Phi(\tau)+\psi,

(124)

which, in turn, yields

\frac{d^{2}u}{d\tau^{2}}+2\sigma\frac{du}{d\tau}+\psi u=0,

(125)

by using the transformation
\Phi=-d(\ln u)/d\tau
. This is a simple second order ODE, where the solutions depend on the sign of the discriminant
\Delta=4\left(\sigma^{2}-\psi\right)=-\left(8+16A+7A^{2}\right)h_{0}^{2}/(1+A)^{2},~A\neq-1
. Taking cases on
\Delta
the solutions for Eq. (
125
) can be combined with the transformation to obtain
\Phi
. If
\Delta>0
,
then

\displaystyle\Phi(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma-\frac{\sqrt{\Delta}}{2}\tanh\left[\frac{\sqrt{\Delta}}{2}\left(\tau-\tau_{0}\right)\right],

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\left(-\frac{\left(4+\sqrt{2}\right)}{7},\frac{\left(-4+\sqrt{2}\right)}{7}\right).

(126)

For
\Delta<0
, the solution of Eq. (
123
) is given by

\displaystyle\Phi(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma+\frac{\sqrt{-\Delta}}{2}\tan\left[\frac{\sqrt{-\Delta}}{2}\left(\tau-\tau_{0}\right)\right],

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\left(-\infty,-\frac{\left(4+\sqrt{2}\right)}{7}\right)\bigcup\left(\frac{\left(-4+\sqrt{2}\right)}{7},+\infty\right).

Finally, for the case
\Delta=0
, we obtain

\Phi(\tau)=\left(1\mp\sqrt{2}\right)h_{0}-\frac{1}{\tau-\tau_{0}},~A=\frac{2}{7}\left(-4\mp\sqrt{2}\right).

(128)

In contrast, if we consider when
\Phi(\tau)=\Phi_{0}
, then (
121
) reads

\frac{dh(\tau)}{d\tau}=-\frac{3+2A}{2+2A}h^{2}(\tau)+\frac{(2+A)\Phi_{0}}{2+2A}h(\tau)-\frac{\Phi_{0}^{2}}{2},

(129)

which reduces to exactly Eq. (
125
) with the transformation
(3+2A)h=(2+2A)d(\ln u)/d\tau
, when
\sigma=-(2+A)\Phi_{0}/(4+4A)
, and
\psi=(3+2A)\Phi_{0}^{2}/(4+4A)
. In this case the discriminant reads
\Delta=\left(4+3A\right)^{2}\Phi_{0}^{2}/(2+2A)^{2},~A\neq-1
. Under the new transformation if
\Delta>0
we get

\displaystyle h(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2+2A}{3+2A}\left(\frac{\sqrt{\Delta}}{2}\tanh\left[\frac{\sqrt{\Delta}}{2}\left(\tau-\tau_{0}\right)\right]-\sigma\right),

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle\left(-\infty,-\frac{4}{3}\right)\bigcup\left(-\frac{4}{3},-1\right)\bigcup\left(-1,+\infty\right).

(130)

Now, since
\Delta
is positive definite there are no solutions for
\Delta<0
and we get the final case
\Delta=0
as

h(\tau)=\Phi_{0}-\frac{2}{\tau-\tau_{0}},~A=-\frac{4}{3}.

(131)

Non-conservative Linear EOS.

The next model is based on assuming an effective equation of state,
\mathchoice{p^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.13435pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.81026pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{eff}}}}{p^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.13435pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.81026pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{eff}}}}{p^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.13435pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.81026pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{eff}}}}{p^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.13435pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.81026pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{eff}}}}=w\mathchoice{\rho^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.13435pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.81026pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{eff}}}}{\rho^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.13435pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.81026pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{eff}}}}{\rho^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.13435pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.81026pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{eff}}}}{\rho^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.16228pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.13435pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.81026pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{eff}}}}
, for some real parameter
w\in\mathbb{R}
. Since the system involves two unknowns
(H,\phi)
, but only a single dynamical equation, an additional condition is required. Alternatively, one could follow the approach of Paiva et. al.
64
and specify a functional form for
\phi(t)
; however, adopting an effective equation of state is a common strategy in modified gravity, and we follow this method here.

Using (
104
) and (
105
) gives the equation

\displaystyle\dot{\phi}

\displaystyle=\phi^{2}-2\phi H-\frac{\alpha}{2}\rho-3w\left(\phi H+\frac{\alpha}{2}\rho\right)

\displaystyle=\phi^{2}-\frac{(32\pi+3\alpha)\phi+3\alpha H}{16\pi+3\alpha}H-3w\frac{16\pi\phi+3\alpha H}{16\pi+3\alpha}H,

(132)

which can easily be made dimensionless via (
120
). Then, combining the dimensionless form of (
132
) with (
121
) and defining a redshift parameter
z
by
1+z=1/a
, gives the system

\displaystyle\frac{d\Phi(z)}{dz}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+z}\bigg(-\frac{\Phi^{2}(z)}{h(z)}+3w\frac{\Phi(z)+Ah(z)}{1+A}

\displaystyle+\frac{2+A}{1+A}\Phi(z)+\frac{A}{1+A}h(z)\bigg),

(133)

\displaystyle\frac{dh(z)}{dz}

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{2+2z}\left(h(z)+w\frac{\Phi(z)+Ah(z)}{1+A}\right),

(134)

where we have made use of the relation

\frac{d}{d\tau}=-(1+z)h(z)\frac{d}{dz}.

(135)

Let us note that in case of vanishing Herglotz field, this system reduces to the single equation

\frac{dh(z)}{dz}=\frac{B}{1+z}h(z),

(136)

where
B=(3\alpha+24\pi)/(3\alpha+16\pi)
. This corresponds to the standard
f(R,T)=R+\alpha T
theory that has been explored extensively, for example in
48
;
82
. The solution to Eq. (
136
) is given by

h(z)=(1+z)^{B},

(137)

which admits the constant deceleration parameter
q=B-1
. This is precisely why this model has traditionally been regarded as inadequate for describing the evolution of the Universe
82
. However, in the presence of a non-vanishing Herglotz field, the deceleration parameter is no longer constant, as we shall see in the following.

IV.2.2
Model II:
f(R,T)=R+\alpha T^{-1}

The next model we consider is a specific case of
f(R,T)=R+\alpha T^{\epsilon}
, where we choose
\epsilon=-1
.

For this model we define
(\tau,h,\Phi,r)
by Eq. (
120
) and let

\alpha=16\pi A\left(\frac{3H_{0}^{2}}{8\pi}\right)^{2}.

(138)

Then, using Eqs. (
102
), (
103
), and a linear effective equation of state we arrive at

\displaystyle h^{2}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Phi(\tau)h(\tau)+r(\tau)-\frac{A}{r(\tau)},

(139)

\displaystyle 2\frac{dh(\tau)}{d\tau}-\frac{d\Phi(\tau)}{d\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\Phi(\tau)h(\tau)-3h^{2}(\tau)

(140)

\displaystyle-\Phi^{2}(\tau)+\frac{3A}{r(\tau)},

\displaystyle\frac{d\Phi(\tau)}{d\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Phi^{2}(\tau)-2\Phi(\tau)h(\tau)-\frac{3A}{r(\tau)}

(141)

\displaystyle-3w\left(\Phi(\tau)h(\tau)-\frac{A}{r(\tau)}\right).

Looking at Eq, (
139
) it is clear that an algebraic constraint on the density function will have two solutions given by

\displaystyle r_{\pm}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\bigg(h(\tau)\left(h(\tau)-\Phi(\tau)\right)

(142)

\displaystyle\pm\sqrt{h^{2}\left(\tau)(h(\tau)-\Phi(\tau)\right)^{2}+4A}\bigg).

Considering both solutions we move into a redshift representation so that the dynamical equations read

\displaystyle\frac{dh}{dz}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2+2z}\left[h+\frac{w}{2}\left(h+\Phi\mp\frac{\sqrt{}}{h}\right)\right],

(143)

\displaystyle\frac{d\Phi}{dz}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+z}\left(-\frac{\Phi^{2}}{h}+5\Phi+\frac{3}{2}\left(w-1\right)\left(h+\Phi\mp\frac{\sqrt{}}{h}\right)\right).

where
\sqrt{}=\sqrt{h^{2}(h-\Phi)^{2}+4A}
.

IV.3
Numerical solutions

Given the complexitiy of the models described by Eqs. (
IV.2.1
)-(
134
) and (
143
)-(
IV.2.2
), we cannot solve them analytically. We therefore resort to numerical techniques. The purpose of this section is to demonstrate that, for a certain range of model parameters, the considered models provide a viable description of the publicly available cosmic chronometer data
62
.
5
5

5

We do not claim to have identified the optimal model parameters; this issue could be addressed in future work.

We proceed as follows. For Models I and II, we integrate the systems (
IV.2.1
)-(
134
) and (
IV.2.2
)-(
143
) numerically using Mathematica, adopting initial conditions
h(0)=1
and
\Phi(0)=\Phi_{0}
, a value to be specified later. Given the numerical solutions for
\Phi(z)
and
h(z)
, the dimensionless matter density for Model I is obtained from Eq. (
122
) as

r(z)=\frac{h^{2}(z)-\Phi(z)h(z)}{1+A}.

(145)

For Model II, the density is determined from (
142
) by selecting the positive root of the resulting expression.

For both models, we also compute the cosmographic parameters: the deceleration parameter

q(z)=(1+z)\frac{1}{h(z)}\frac{dh(z)}{dz}-1,

(146)

the jerk parameter

j(z)=q(z)(2q(z)+1)+(1+z)\frac{dq(z)}{dz},

(147)

and the snap parameter

s(z)=\frac{j(z)-1}{3\left(q(z)-\frac{1}{2}\right)}.

(148)

These quantities allow us to analyze the cosmographic behavior of the models. Furthermore, to assess whether the models lie in the quintessence or phantom regimes, or permit phantom crossing, we evaluate the
Om(z)
diagnostic

Om(z)=\frac{h^{2}(z)-1}{(1+z)^{3}-1}.

(149)

exhibits a positive slope at late times (typically for
z\lesssim 0.5
, depending on parameter choices), suggesting a quintessence-like behavior, and a negative slope at higher redshifts (
z\gtrsim 1
), indicative of a phantom-like regime. For the inverse model, the evolution of
Om(z)
strongly depends on the chosen parameters; therefore, a decisive assessment requires comparison with additional datasets and strict constraints on the model parameters.

. In

; deviations indicate additional dynamical effects and departures from standard cosmology. In the Herglotz-type models,

differs from this constant, with the linear and inverse models behaving differently over redshift. For the present day, the linear model predicts

or

or

.

The snap parameter
s(z)
, involving higher derivatives of the scale factor, provides insight into the cosmological evolution. It is particularly relevant at redshifts close to
z\simeq 2
, where expansions in powers of
z
require terms beyond linear and quadratic. In the Herglotz-type
f(R,T)
models,
s(z)
differs noticeably from the
\Lambda
CDM expectation (see Fig.
7
). Some models show a transition from negative to positive values, while others remain negative over the entire redshift range. The present-day values are predicted as
s(0)\simeq-0.05
or
-0.10
for the linear model, and
s(0)\simeq-0.38
or
0.62
for the inverse model, with
s(0)\approx 0
also possible. As with the jerk parameter, these values are sensitive to the initial condition
\Phi(0)=\Phi_{0}
.

V
Discussion and final remarks

Dissipative effects, such as viscosity and heat transfer, are essential in many physical processes. Most cosmological models, however, assume a perfect or non-dissipative fluid, an approximation that fails at high pressures or densities. Processes such as matter–radiation interactions, particle production, photon transport, and the dynamics of cosmic strings or quark–gluon plasma require dissipative dynamics. A natural extension of the matter term in the gravitational field equations is therefore the inclusion of dissipative contributions in the energy-momentum tensor, which in turn influence the gravitational field.

Quantum systems can also exhibit dissipative behavior arising from interactions with their environment
19
. A primary example is the energy decay of a thermodynamic system, often modeled as the result of irreversible energy exchange with a thermal bath. However, some decay processes occur without direct coupling to a bath and require alternative descriptions.

On the other hand one cannot a priori neglect the possibility that the gravitational field, and the associated geometry, are in fact dissipative systems, whose behavior are described by the second law of thermodynamics. Such a situation may be possible if one assumes that gravity is derived from an entropic action that couples matter fields and geometry
14
. The entropic action can be defined as the quantum relative entropy between the metric of spacetime and the metric induced by the matter fields.

Although dissipation is well known in nature, providing a Lagrangian description of dissipative phenomena presents important mathematical and physical challenges. In particular, one must distinguish between two types of Lagrangians: physical (standard) and mathematical (non-standard). A physical Lagrangian is expressed as the difference between kinetic and potential energy, while a mathematical Lagrangian reproduces the correct equations of motion without having this standard form.

As a simple example, consider a damped oscillator describing a particle of mass
m
moving in an external potential
U(x)
under friction. The equation of motion Eq. (
13
) can also be derived from the physical Lagrangian

L=e^{\nu t}\left(\frac{1}{2}m\dot{x}^{2}-U(x)\right).

(151)

On the other hand the equation
\ddot{x}+\nu\dot{x}=0,
describing the motion of a free particle in the presence of friction only, can be obtained from both the physical (standard) Lagrangian

L=\frac{1}{2}e^{\nu t}\dot{x}^{2},

(152)

and from the non-physical Lagrangians
23

L=\frac{1}{\left(e^{2\nu t}\dot{x}+e^{\nu t}\right)},L=\dot{x}\ln\left|\dot{x}\right|-\nu x,L=\left(\dot{x}^{k}+e^{-k\nu t}\right)^{1/k},

(153)

for some constant
k\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
.

Hence, there are several ways to formulate a Lagrangian description of dissipative systems. In this work, we adopt a variational approach based on the Herglotz generalization of the standard Euler-Lagrange principle. Although the Herglotz Lagrangian could in principle depend nonlinearly on the action, it remains a (generalized) physical Lagrangian, typically expressed as the difference between a kinetic term and a generalized potential. For example, the Herglotz Lagrangian for a damped particle, Eq. (
12
) yields the same equation of motion as Eq. (
151
) and can be written as
L=T-U_{\rm eff}(x,S)
, with
U_{\rm eff}(x,S)=U(x)+(\nu/m)S
. The existence of a physical Lagrangian greatly simplifies the interpretation of the corresponding models.

In this work, we have reviewed the Herglotz variational principle in classical mechanics and various field theories, including general relativity, and applied it to a generalized
f(R,T)
-type gravity action. This yields field equations similar to standard
f(R,T)
gravity, but with additional contributions from the Herglotz vector. The divergence of the energy-momentum tensor was derived and found to be nonzero, as is typical in geometry–matter coupling models. However, energy-momentum conservation can be restored through a natural constraint on the Herglotz field, providing a unique mechanism to maintain generality while exploring conserved geometry–matter couplings. A natural next step would be to extend other
f(R,\text{Matter})

63
;
61
;
46
;
84
;
45
;
27
theories using the Herglotz variational approach and assess whether it yields similar improvements.

The Newtonian limit of the theory was also explored by deriving the modified Poisson equation and analyzing the motion of massive particles. In the former, corrections to the Newtonian potential were obtained for the specific cases of a constant and radial inverse square Herglotz field. The magnitude of the Herglotz field in these two cases was then constrained by considering the perihelion shift of Mercury. Additionally, the deflection angle of light around a massive body was found to scale as the square of the wavelength. In fact, this matches the scaling described by light passing through plasma which has been observed recently. Therefore, one could interpret the Herglotz field as a possible alternative description to wavelength dependent deflection near massive bodies.

We analyzed the cosmology of Herglotz-type
f(R,T)
gravity for a perfect fluid in a dust FLRW Universe, where the Herglotz vector reduces to a time-dependent scalar function. The generalized FLRW equations were derived, and to close the system, two forms of
f(R,T)
were considered. For
f(R,T)=R+\alpha T
, two analytical solutions were found, corresponding to a de Sitter-like universe and a constant Herglotz function. An effective equation of state was introduced for comparison with the non-Herglotz case. Numerical solutions, with appropriately chosen parameter values, were shown to fit Hubble data while producing an accelerating universe, a feature absent in the non-Herglotz scenario. The model
f(R,T)=R+\alpha T^{-1}
was also studied using a linear effective equation of state, yielding a more complex system that required a numerical treatment.

In conclusion, the Herglotz variational extension of
f(R,T)
gravity provides a promising framework for describing dissipative effects in gravitational systems across different scales. It also offers a good match to observational cosmological data, being able to reproduce the predictions of the
\Lambda
CDM model. However, before one could give a fair assessment of the physical and cosmological viability of Herglotz-type
f(R,T)
models, a detailed comparison with existing datasets is necessary. From a theoretical perspective, further studies are needed to clarify the physical origin of the Herglotz vector and its connection to dissipation mechanisms at both classical and quantum levels. Obtaining analytic solutions in spherical symmetry, as well as exploring gravitational waves in Herglotz-type
f(R,\text{Matter})
theories, would be natural topics for follow-up works.

Taking into account the results of this work, Herglotz-type
f(R,T)
gravity and the corresponding cosmological models could provide a compelling theoretical alternative to the standard
\Lambda
CDM model and general relativity. Although further work is required to clarify some of its theoretical foundations, the theory shows significant potential in explaining observational data and improving on models that were unrealistic under the standard Hamiltonian approach.
